# 🎬 Should Designers Listen to Negative Feedback? — Game Maker's Toolkit


### Лёгкие режимы и «мягкие» опции расширяют аудиторию  ·  [03:29](https://youtu.be/P05ONfLOqmY?t=209)
**Мысль:** Игры добавляли безопасные режимы, чекпоинты и ползунки сложности после отзывов; разработчик Pathologic 2 назвал неправильным исключать людей, не способных тренировать реакцию.

> Feedback can also be used to make games more accessible to a wider audience. The game SOMA, for example, scared some players away due to its spooky survival horror gameplay - so Frictional released an update called "Safe Mode" which let players disable all the baddies and just focus on the storyline. Elsewhere, Yooka Laylee and the Impossible Lair was made significantly less, well, impossible, with an update that adds checkpoints to its hardest level. The atmospheric roguelike Below just got a new mode called "Explore" which turns off the game's most challenging aspects. And even the notoriously unfriendly Pathologic 2 can now be made easier, thanks to difficulty sliders - with one of the game's devs noting that it was "not right on a fundamental level" to leave out players who were unable to improve their twitch skills or sense of time.

**Для Напарника:** Дать Фокусу мягкие режимы (короче сессия, пауза без штрафа) без пометки «упрощённый» и без стыда.



# 🎬 So you want to make an Incremental Game? — Aarimous


### Сделай раз и делегируй: не улучшай утомительный клик  ·  [08:16](https://youtu.be/S-JsFaJhs88?t=496)
**Мысль:** Клики утомляют; в Norp показывают график DPS, чтобы игрок видел, что клики почти ничего не дают, и не дают апгрейдов клику. Иначе оптимальная стратегия — как можно быстрее мучить кнопку.

> Another area I'm seeing a lot of success in and I find interesting is do it once then delegate.

**Для Напарника:** Не награждать за ручные повторные тапы или скорость: награда идёт за присутствие и завершённые шаги, а не за механическое повторение.



# 🎬 Storytelling masterclass for designers — Dive Club 🤿


### Микро-конфликты: эмоциональный пик, где пользователю тревожно  ·  [07:09](https://youtu.be/LZEh-QTim2Q?t=429)
**Мысль:** Момент тревоги или перегруза в потоке можно сделать завязкой истории: сначала мир пользователя, затем конфликт, затем решение.

> Inciting incidents exist in the micro too maybe there's an unexpected change in the data or maybe you identify an emotional high point where users feel anxious or overwhelmed in a given flow now you can use that as a way to tell a story that makes your designs more memorable first you create the user World beforehand then you really highlight that moment of conflict and then you can propose Your solution so going back to our Q3 planning meeting internalizing this moment of conflict is what empowers your product lead to effectively tell your story if you arm your person with the major piece of conflict and the way to overcome it they are more able to rearticulate that idea onwards.

**Для Напарника:** Использовать момент, где человеку с СДВГ тревожно перед стартом, как отправную точку для онбординга и текстов.



# 🎬 The ADHD Executive Function Map Nobody Gave You (+8 AI Prompts) — Kat Tan | Technically ADHD


### Инициация: дело не в мотивации, а в системе награды; снизь трение  ·  [00:33](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=33)
**Мысль:** Без срочности, новизны или интереса мозг не видит стимула; стыд за не-начало лишь увеличивает потребность в дофамине. Решение — уменьшить трение, шаг до 2 минут и без решений.

> Most people think ADHD is a focus issue when it's actually a focus management issue. Getting focused is not a motivation problem, it's a neurological problem with the brain's reward system and lower baseline dopamine levels. Without urgency, novelty, or genuine interest, the brain can't see the incentive to start a task. On top of that, the more you shame yourself for not starting, the more dopamine you need to overcome the extra negative emotions. So, some ADHD specific advice. Just do it is meaningless for ADHD brains. The issue isn't effort. Instead, reduce friction and stack dopamine hits to make starting a no-brainer. You can also use this AI prompt. Break the task into the smallest possible first step, under 2 minutes, zero decisions required. Give me that step then the one immediately after it. Both steps should be so specific I can start without thinking.

**Для Напарника:** Первый микрошаг в api/breakdown: меньше 2 минут, ноль решений, сразу следующий; без стыда за пропуск.


### Слепота ко времени: делай время видимым, обратный таймлайн с буфером 30%  ·  [01:27](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=87)
**Мысль:** 10 минут ощущаются как 2 часа, дедлайн через 2 недели не ощущается; планировщиков мало, нужно сделать время осязаемым.

> Dr. K from HealthyGamerGG says time blindness is >> the thing that causes the most damage to my patients' lives that they are unaware of as a problem with ADHD. >> You might think you're always late because you were lazy or didn't try hard enough to be on time when it's actually a neurological gap in how time is felt. 10 minutes can feel the same as 2 hours. A deadline 2 weeks away then becomes meaningless. The point is you cannot possibly manage time if you aren't even aware of it. So, some ADHD specific advice, more planners, calendar apps, and reminders alone aren't going to cut it. Find ways to make time visible or tangible and train your time perception. An AI prompt for this one, build a backward timeline for a task due by the deadline. I can work X hours a day on this. Add a 30% time buffer to every step. Format as specific time blocks and not as step list. The result should be a complete day-by-day schedule I can follow without further planning.

**Для Напарника:** Таймер Фокуса и кольцо прогресса как визуализация времени; в плане использовать буфер +30%.


### Сначала легкое — потому что только его можно удержать в рабочей памяти  ·  [02:25](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=145)
**Мысль:** При слабой рабочей памяти человек берёт простое, не важное; совет — выбрать одно самое важное дело и забыть об остальном.

> Overwhelm doesn't come from laziness. It comes from an overloaded brain unable to sort by importance. When everything feels equally urgent, it's easy to become paralyzed by the endless list of options. Your brain has low working memory, so you do the easiest task first. But only because it's the only task you can actually hold the full picture in your head, not because you should be doing it next. ADHD advice, you already know what to do. You don't always need a three-month plan. Identify the most important one thing to do next. Forget about everything else. And from AI, here's everything on my plate. List everything. My main goal this week is X. I have Y hours today. Rank these by which one of three tasks to move me closest to that goal. List what to skip entirely today. Start with what I should do first.

**Для Напарника:** Дом показывает один следующий шаг, остальные спрятаны (Hick's Law).


### Удержание внимания: интерес-мозг, вызов с чётким условием победы  ·  [03:12](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=192)
**Мысль:** Мозг, не способный сидеть над письмом 5 минут, 6 часов сидит над хобби; нужны челленджи, мелкие награды и партнёры.

> Even if you've managed to start a task or this video, the next step of executive functioning is to continue. You realize you're going to have to sit still for hours to get it done. You get bored. However, you know the same brain that can't focus on a work email for five minutes can focus on a fun hobby for six hours. The brain is interest driven. Until you get the natural dopamine hit from making incremental progress on a goal, continuing to focus will feel like a chore. Some ADHD specific advice, tap into challenges, bite-size rewards, and accountability partners to sustain momentum. Make everything more fun. A tip from AI, reframe the task as a specific challenge with a clear win condition that makes it feel like a game. Give me one concrete question or angle to focus on for the next 25 minutes. The output should feel like a mission briefing, not a to-do item.

**Для Напарника:** Сессия Фокуса подаётся как миссия на 25 минут с ясным условием победы, а не пункт списка.


### Консистентность: минимальное недельное действие и структурное препятствие пропуску  ·  [04:02](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=242)
**Мысль:** Дисциплина не работает; нужна система, которая мостом соединяет всплески мотивации: минимум до 30 минут и структура.

> Almost every ADHD brain I talked to has a consistency problem. You get really excited about something for a week, but after the shiny object syndrome wears off, you're left with a graveyard of unfinished project. For a dopamine-driven brain, interest, urgency, challenge, and novelty lead the way. As a result, showing up week after week necessary but boring work is genuinely harder for an ADHD brain. Some ADHD specific advice, telling yourself you just need to be more disciplined isn't going to work. You need a system to bridge momentum between pockets of motivation. With some help from AI, I care about project or goal, but I keep losing momentum after the initial excitement fades. I can realistically spend X hours a week on it even on low motivation weeks. Give me one, a minimum weekly action under 30 minutes that keeps progress moving without requiring peak energy, and two, one structural change that makes it harder to skip.

**Для Напарника:** Остров растёт даже от минимальной сессии; минимальное действие недели без стрика-наказания.


### Избегание — эмоциональная проблема: назови эмоцию, дай шаг до 5 минут  ·  [04:57](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=297)
**Мысль:** Избегание задач часто вытекает из стыда/страха критики; полезно назвать эмоцию, одним предложением переформулировать и выбрать безопасное действие.

> Along with time blindness, this is one of the most underestimated aspects of ADHD. Research shows ADHD brains tend to have an overactive amygdala, the brain structure primarily responsible for emotion regulation. Frustration hits harder. Criticism lands deeper. This makes it harder to complete tasks and significantly impacts relationships when it comes to facing rejection or failure. So, some ADHD advice, you may be avoiding tasks or hard conversations because it's an emotional problem, not a logical one. Identify the negative emotions behind the anxiety and take a few breaths before trying to brute force it. An AI prompt for this one, reframe why I'm avoiding task or conversation. My best guess at the emotion behind it is something like dread, shame, or fear of criticism. Give me one, a one-sentence reframe that reduces the emotional charge, and two, one action under five minutes that feels emotionally safe enough to start.

**Для Напарника:** Если шаг не идёт, напарник тепло спрашивает: «что тут страшно?» и предлагает пятиминутный безопасный старт.


### Умение остановиться: записка «будущему мне» при переходе между задачами  ·  [05:46](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=346)
**Мысль:** Переключение требует отпустить, переориентироваться и сбросить состояние; шаблон «где остановился, какой следующий шаг, вопросы» упрощает возврат.

> Every time you switch tasks, your brain needs to actively let go of the last thing, reorient yourself onto the next thing, and reset your entire mental state. On the flip side, you might experience hyperfocus. When you're not locked in, your output can skyrocket. But, you can also lose track of everything else. Time, hunger, important messages, and also that meeting that you were supposed to be in. ADHD tip for this one, train your ability not just to start tasks, but also to stop. Build structure around transition so you can exit when you need to. Here's the AI prompt. Give me a four future me template for a current task that I can fill in within X minutes. It should capture where I left off, the exact next step when I return, and any open questions. Keep it to three to four fields max.

**Для Напарника:** Перед выходом из Фокуса напарник сохраняет «где ты остановился» и предлагает следующий шаг в очереди.


### Когнитивная импульсивность: паркуй идею вместо того, чтобы хвататься  ·  [06:28](https://youtu.be/A5XOw_jsNss?t=388)
**Мысль:** Согласие или запуск нового проекта до завершения прежнего; помогает пауза и вопросы про приоритет, цену отсрочки.

> ADHD impulsivity isn't just about interrupting or acting recklessly. It's more often about cognitive impulsivity. Saying yes before considering your bandwidth or starting a new project before finishing the last one. Between more intense emotions, the constant dopamine chasing, and everything feeling urgent, it's easy to make snap decisions before considering consequences. Advice for those with ADHD, it's okay to take a pause, even for five extra seconds. Train your awareness that your capacity is limited and not everything has to happen right now. And the last AI prompt, evaluate whether I should act on this idea now. Describe it. My current top priority is X. Answer three questions. One, does it align with or pull me away from X? Two, what would I have to pause or drop to pursue it? Three, is there a real cost to deciding in two weeks instead of today? And end with one recommendation, pursue now, park for later, or drop it.

**Для Напарника:** Кнопка «припарковать идею» в чате, чтобы не отвлекаться из сессии, без ощущения потери.



# 🎬 The Best Landing Page Structure for 2026 — Arsh Sanwarwala | ThrillX


### Ключевое возражение получает отдельную секцию; фича + выгода в одном заголовке  ·  [14:42](https://youtu.be/vwhR2M0SQtI?t=882)
**Мысль:** Главный страх покупателя (будет ли больно, долго ли) заслуживает своей секции; заголовок объединяет функцию и выгоду.

> Painfree 10-minute treatment for a lifetime of clear vision. This was one of the more critical value props, which is the fact that it's painfree cuz a ton of people have anxiety around is it actually going to hurt? Is how fast is it going to be? I feel like it's going to take forever. So, this deserved its own separate section. Same thing with this one over here. Relax with safety first care and ongoing support for full peace of mind. But we had smaller value props that you know were not as critical. Like for example, no more low light halos. You know, only clinic in the UK offering rate tracing. See better than ever with superior 2012 vision and virtual eye modeling for precise and safe surgery. When we go back to the headlines, right? Fast and pain-free 10-minute treatment, right? That is the feature, but the benefit is a lifetime of clear vision. So, we're combining both into one.

**Для Напарника:** Выделить блок про главный страх Напарника («опять не получится / будет давить») и ответить на него тёплой формулировкой.



# 🎬 The BEST Productivity Method Ever for ADHD | Interstitial Journaling — Novie by the Sea


### Почему системы не держатся: слишком строгие душат, слишком свободные забываются  ·  [01:06](https://youtu.be/UFidZJhxz84?t=66)
**Мысль:** Планирование будущего не работает для мозга без чувства будущего; нужен инструмент, работающий в моменте и дающий ясность сразу.

> So, I've tried so many productivity methods over the years. All the planners, all the habit trackers, Pomodoros, time blocking, Notion templates, all of it. And I swear some of these are built by people who have perfect time awareness, perfect executive function, and perfect lives. Because for me, nothing ever stuck. If the system is too strict, I suffocate. I instantly feel like I'm in a box, and then I rebel and stop doing it after 2 days. But if a system is too loose, then I forget about it completely. And if it requires planning things in the future, then it's even worse. My brain has absolutely no understanding of future. If you ask me what I'm doing in 3 hours, I don't know. I barely know what I'm doing in 3 minutes. So, I needed something that works in the moment. Something that doesn't require planning or predicting or scheduling or mapping out an entire day. Something I can pick up in the middle of the mess, and it gives me clarity immediately.

**Для Напарника:** Дом и Фокус должны работать «здесь и сейчас», без планирования дня вперёд; план на сегодня не обязателен.


### Интерстициальный журнал: записывай, что делаешь сейчас и что дальше  ·  [02:11](https://youtu.be/UFidZJhxz84?t=131)
**Мысль:** Короткие записи между задачами дают понимание, где ты, что только что сделал и что следующее.

> So, what even is interstitial journaling? The name sounds fancy, but it's literally just this. You write down what you're doing as you're doing it in between tasks. That's it. You don't plan, you don't write it to do list, and you don't try to guess the next 10 things. You simply document what you're doing right now, in the moment, and the next tiny thing you're going to do. It can look like this. Little entries, short sentences, just you narrating your day as you move through it. It feels stupidly simple, but it works for an ADHD brain. You always know where you are. You always know what you just did. You always know what you're going into next. No guessing and no what was I doing confusion.

**Для Напарника:** Лента чата в Доме как журнал: напарник коротко фиксирует «что сделали» и «что дальше».


### Журнал сбрасывает мозг в настоящее, учит чувству времени и снимает стыд  ·  [03:09](https://youtu.be/UFidZJhxz84?t=189)
**Мысль:** Запись шага даёт микро-подотчётность, возвращает к последнему действию, метки времени тренируют ощущение часа, а список сделанного снимает стыд.

> ADHD makes you forget things instantly. Like you walk into a room, and suddenly you're like a lost toddler in a mall. You don't even know why you walked in there. You're like, "What was I doing? Why am I here?" And here is why interstitial journaling helps. It resets your brain every time you pause. Instead of spiraling into distractions, the journal pulls you back to the present moment. It's like a built-in checkpoint, and it creates micro accountability. And not in a stressful way. It's just that when you write, "I'll do laundry," you are 10 times more likely to actually do it. You know that feeling when you feel like you are suddenly behind on everything, but you can't even list what you were supposed to do? When you journal your steps, the fog lifts, and you see your day clearly. And it teaches your brain the one thing that it struggles with, which is time awareness. ADHD time is weird. Everything is either now or not now. With timestamps, your brain starts understanding what an hour feels like, what 20 minutes feels like, and so on, without even trying. And it keeps you moving. Even if you get distracted, you can always look back at your last entry and go, "All right, I was working on this project." And then you just continue working on that. And lastly, it reduces shame because you can see that you actually did things, even on days when you felt like you didn't.

**Для Напарника:** Показывать «что ты сделал сегодня» на Доме (ориентиры, история сессий) как доказательство прогресса в дни, когда кажется, что ничего не сделано.


### Паралич от «не знаю с чего начать» и «слишком большое»: сожми до самого маленького шага  ·  [05:03](https://youtu.be/UFidZJhxz84?t=303)
**Мысль:** Вместо «работать над проектом» пишем «открыть папку проекта, просто посмотреть»; крошечный шаг тянет следующий.

> So, interstitial journaling is insanely good when you feel overwhelmed and frozen because ADHD paralysis comes from two things. One, not knowing where to start, and two, feeling like everything is too big. But journaling forces you to shrink everything down to the smallest next step. You don't write, "Work on the project." You write, "Open the project folder, just look around." And that tiny step naturally pulls you into the next one. No pressure, no perfection, just movement.

**Для Напарника:** Прямой образец для /api/breakdown: первый шаг должен быть почти смешно маленьким («открыть файл»).


### Система, в которую можно вернуться после перерыва без вины  ·  [06:22](https://youtu.be/UFidZJhxz84?t=382)
**Мысль:** Дисциплина быстро выгорает; рабочие системы допускают пропуск недели и возвращение одной строкой.

> And you don't need discipline for this. People always assume productivity requires discipline. But with ADHD, discipline burns out fast. What actually works is systems you can fall back into even after you forget about them. And interstitial journaling is exactly that. You can drop it for a week, and then come back to it on a random Tuesday at 4:00 p.m. Write one line, and you're back.

**Для Напарника:** Возвращение после паузы: приветствие напарника без упрёка, остров на месте, достаточно одного шага.


### Структура + гибкость + мгновенная обратная связь = как друг, идущий рядом  ·  [07:41](https://youtu.be/UFidZJhxz84?t=461)
**Мысль:** СДВГ-мозгу нужны три вещи одновременно: структура, гибкость и немедленный отклик; такой инструмент ощущается как друг рядом.

> Why I think ADHD people love it. Because it's not planning. It's not productivity culture. It's not strict or aesthetic or high-maintenance. It's just a way to stay connected to yourself. So, ADHD brains need three things: structure, flexibility, and instant feedback. Interstitial journaling gives all three at the same time. It keeps you organized, but not restricted. And it feels like having a little friend walking through the day with you.

**Для Напарника:** Формулировка позиционирования напарника: организует, но не ограничивает; даёт мгновенный отклик.



# 🎬 The Design of Everyday Things   Made with Clipchamp — Vorldrevolution


### Поток, выученная беспомощность и «нет провалов, есть обучение»  ·  [04:01](https://youtu.be/VX_I-ydAUWU?t=241)
**Мысль:** Поток это задача в меру сложная с постоянным прогрессом; если действия повторно не дают результата, человек сдаётся (выученная беспомощность), а общество учит искать виноватого.

> Flow is where we are challenged enough but not too much coupled with continual progress what matters is an individual's perception not whether or not causal relationships exist learned helplessness is when someone does something that repeatedly does yield the desired result so they give up and stop trying all together at the extreme it can lead to depression and difficulty coping with daily life there is no failure only learning experiences Society teaches us that when something goes wrong someone is to blame whether ourselves or someone else.

**Для Напарника:** Подтверждает тон без вины: нет провала, есть шаг; маленькие шаги дают ощутимый результат, чтобы не копить беспомощность.



# 🎬 The Father of Industrial Design | Dieter Rams | Design Docs #industrialDesign #Design#designhistory — Design Docs


### Простота это не минимализм, а психологическая ясность  ·  [05:35](https://youtu.be/v7ogCO9CJQs?t=335)
**Мысль:** Рамс создаёт атмосферу порядка, позволяющую людям сосредоточиться на жизни, а не на инструментах; эмоциональная связь с вещью не противоречит строгости.

> Despite his reputation for precision, Rams was not a cold rationalist. He understood that people form emotional connections with objects they use daily. His design sought to create an atmosphere of order that allowed people to focus on their lives rather than their tools. To him, simplicity was not aesthetic minimalism. It was psychological clarity.

**Для Напарника:** Для людей с СДВГ чистота интерфейса должна снижать когнитивную нагрузку, но оставаться тёплой: минимум элементов, но с характером кота и острова.



# 🎬 The INSANE Gap Between Average and World-Class UX/UI Design — uxpeak


### Цена информации: решить, что помогает решению, а что только шум  ·  [14:56](https://youtu.be/U7jTCgIWS4w?t=896)
**Мысль:** Каждая добавленная информация делает интерфейс занятнее, поэтому нужен критерий: помогает ли она принять решение.

> The trade-off is that all this extra content makes the interface busier. So, the challenge is knowing what information genuinely helps the decision and what just adds noise.

**Для Напарника:** Критерий для любого нового элемента на Доме/Фокусе: помогает ли он сделать следующий шаг прямо сейчас. Если нет, не добавлять (Hick's Law).



# 🎬 The Laws of UX - 19 Psychological Design Principles — Joseph Todaro


### Закон Хика: время решения растёт с числом вариантов  ·  [01:52](https://youtu.be/fYs2Mdyasuc?t=112)
**Мысль:** Упрощай выбор, разбивая сложную задачу на маленькие шаги; заваленный выбором человек вынужден работать.

> Hix law the time it takes to make a decision increases with the number of and complexity of choices ideally you should simplify choices for the user by breaking down complex tasks into smaller steps in 1952 William Edmund hick and Ray Hyman set out to examine the relationship between the number of stimuli present and an individual's reaction time to any given stimulus as you would expect the more stimuli to choose from the longer it takes for the user to make a decision on which one to interact with users bombarded with choices have to take time to interpret and decide giving them work they don't want.

**Для Напарника:** Подтверждает дробление задачи на микрошаги и ограниченный набор длительностей.


### Закон Паркинсона: задача раздувается до отведённого времени  ·  [06:38](https://youtu.be/fYs2Mdyasuc?t=398)
**Мысль:** Работа занимает всё выделенное время.

> Parkinson's law any task will inflate until all of the available time is spent.

**Для Напарника:** Обоснование коротких таймбокс-сессий (25 минут) вместо открытого времени.


### Эффект Зейгарник: незавершённое запоминается лучше  ·  [09:07](https://youtu.be/fYs2Mdyasuc?t=547)
**Мысль:** Прерванные задачи держатся в памяти лучше завершённых (Зейгарник, 1920-е).

> People remember uncompleted or interrupted tasks better than completed tasks bloomer will fall 'no Zeigarnik was a soviet psychologist and psychiatrist in the 1920s she conducted a study on memory in which she compared memory in relation to incomplete and complete tasks she had found that incomplete tasks are easier to remember than successful ones.

**Для Напарника:** Подтверждает очередь шагов (saveStepQueue): предлагать следующим «Следующий шаг» без давления.

> And the Zeigarnik effect, from Bluma Zeigarnik's research in the 1920s, found that people remember unfinished tasks far better than completed ones. Any agent that fails to clearly mark an incomplete multi-step task is throwing away a psychological hook that could keep you oriented across a long session.

# 🎬 The Man Who Ate Lunch with Steve Jobs Every Day — Newsthink


### Шарик на ручке «просто чтобы было чем заняться»: дизайн для рук  ·  [01:59](https://youtu.be/FqhfFNTtuv0?t=119)
**Мысль:** Люди любят вертеть вещи в руках, и бесполезная на вид деталь очеловечивает технологию: хочется трогать и играть.

> When he had to design a pen for a Japanese client, he noticed that people liked to “fiddle” around with their pens. So he added a ball-and-clip mechanism to the top of the pen that served no purpose other than to give someone something to do. This humanized the technology. People didn’t want to just write with the pen – they wanted to touch it and play around with it. Much like how they “play” with their Apple products today.

**Для Напарника:** Для СДВГ: безопасный «фиджет» в интерфейсе (мягко откликающийся кот или огонь, на тап реагирует без награды и счётчиков) при условии, что не нарушает диегетичности.


### iMac: прозрачность и ручка, чтобы не бояться; «i» как individual  ·  [09:49](https://youtu.be/FqhfFNTtuv0?t=589)
**Мысль:** Люди нервничали рядом с компьютерами, и цель была сделать их доступными и не пугающими: прозрачный корпус и ручка создают отношения, потому что то, чего боишься, не трогаешь.

> Ive loved the idea. “A lot of people at that point in time were nervous around computers, around technology. So, our clear goal was how we can make the product accessible and not intimidating.” Translucency made it less intimidating. Ive also added a handle to the computer. This was not for carrying but to build a bond with the user, to make it seem more approachable. He explained to Isaacson: “If you’re scared of something, then you won’t touch it. I could see my mum being scared to touch it. So I thought, if there’s this handle on it, it makes a relationship possible.” The importance of a personal connection was reflected in the name of the computer. The “i” in iMac stood for internet but it also stood for individual.

**Для Напарника:** Для тревожной в начале задачи аудитории: тёплый, мягкий, «подержаться можно» стиль (кот, закруглённые формы), снижающий страх прикосновения к приложению.

# 🎬 The Psychology of High Converting Landing Pages — Arsh Sanwarwala | ThrillX


### Когнитивная лёгкость: приоритет главного, иконки/буллеты, простой язык  ·  [04:11](https://youtu.be/qGJG7nLPd8w?t=251)
**Мысль:** Порог когнитивной нагрузки: меньше выбора и текста, чёткая иерархия размером, весом и цветом, язык 5–8 класса.

> The third principle is about making things easy, aka cognitive load. The human brain has something called a cognitive threshold, which just means that there's a limit to how much the human brain can process at one given time. A low-converting landing page has a high cognitive load, which means that people have to exert a lot of mental power just to decipher the information on the page, right? Because maybe the page has a confusing layout, it has way too much information, or just way too many choices. As a result, people end up getting frustrated and overwhelmed and just end up exiting the page altogether without ever converting. What you want to aim for instead is called cognitive ease. This is a landing page where a human can process the information with little to no effort. High-converting landing pages achieve this by prioritizing the most important conversion elements and completely getting rid of anything else. They avoid having large blocks of text by using things like scannable icons and headings or bullet points that are way easier to digest. They have crystal-clear messaging that speaks on a fifth or eighth-grade reading level, whereas poor landing pages use vague headlines or generic jargon. And finally, high-converting pages have clear visual hierarchy, which is basically the art of guiding a user's information from the most important elements to the least important through things like font size, weight, color, etc.

**Для Напарника:** Особенно важно для СДВГ-аудитории: на каждом экране один главный элемент и минимум текста.



# 🎬 The Real Reason You Can't Put Your Phone Down — Kohi Reels


### Мозг не сломан: древняя машина нацелена на стеклянный прямоугольник; опыты Скиннера  ·  [00:33](https://youtu.be/kdMc7PyBpbo?t=33)
**Мысль:** Голуби при случайном подкреплении долбили рычаг упорнее, чем при предсказуемом; переменное подкрепление — один из сильнейших паттернов поведения.

> Here's the reframe. Your brain isn't broken. It's working exactly the way it was built to work millions of years before screens existed. The problem is that something figured out how to aim that ancient machinery at a glass rectangle in your pocket. To understand this, you have to go back to a lab in the 1950s. A psychologist named B.F. Skinner was studying pigeons. He put them in small boxes with a lever. Peck the lever, get a food pellet. Simple enough. But, then Skinner changed one thing. Instead of giving food every single time the lever was pecked, he gave it randomly. Sometimes after one peck, sometimes after 20. No pattern the pigeon could predict. What happened next surprised him. The pigeons didn't peck less because the reward was unreliable. They pecked more. Obsessively more. Pigeons on a predictable schedule would peck, eat, and walk away satisfied. Pigeons on a random schedule would peck for hours, almost unable to stop. Skinner called this a variable ratio reinforcement schedule. And it turns out to be one of the most powerful behavior patterns ever discovered.

**Для Напарника:** Рамка без стыда для текстов: «твой мозг работает как задумано, а среда его перехватила».


### Тяга проверять — не слабость, а древний контур поиска; названная тяга теряет автоматизм  ·  [05:29](https://youtu.be/kdMc7PyBpbo?t=329)
**Мысль:** Неопределённая награда в природе означала стоящую цель; назвать позыв («это сработал контур предсказания») на секунду достаточно, чтобы вернуть выбор.

> None of this requires you to be weak-willed, distracted by nature, or addicted to your phone specifically. The exact same neural circuitry would respond the exact same way to any device built on the same principle. Your ancestors needed this system to survive. Uncertain rewards in nature usually meant something worth pursuing. A berry bush that wasn't always full, a hunting ground that wasn't always productive, a watering hole that wasn't always safe. The brain that got excited about maybe was the brain that kept searching instead of giving up. And that searching kept your ancestors alive in an unpredictable world. That circuitry never updated for smartphones. It didn't need to. It was never broken in the first place. It's just being pulled hundreds of times a day by something engineered specifically to trigger it. Think about what that means the next time you feel that pull to check, even when you know there's probably nothing there. That feeling isn't weakness. It's a few hundred thousand years of evolution responding exactly the way it evolved to respond to a stimulus your ancestors never could have imagined and never had to resist. So, what do you actually do with this? Not turn off notifications and hope, although that helps. The deeper shift is recognizing the moment itself. When you feel the urge to check, you're not making a choice about information. You're feeling a small, ancient prediction circuit fire. The same one that once kept your ancestors searching for food in an unpredictable world. Naming that feeling for what it is, just for a second, is often enough to loosen its grip because the pull only works automatically when it goes unnoticed.

**Для Напарника:** Реплика кота при отвлечении: «заметил(а), что рука потянулась?» — мягкое называние импульса без осуждения.

### Виртуальные рутины с низким вниманием: можно сочетать с аудиокнигой или видео  ·  [04:42](https://youtu.be/n3UwMqLukdw?t=282)
**Мысль:** Люди любят рутину, особенно сочетаемую с чем-то ещё; задачи с малым умственным напряжением не расходуют энергию и позволяют отдыхать, играя.

> Humans naturally crave a routine, especially one that can be combined with something else. Because these virtual chores require very little mental focus, we treat them exactly like real-life chores. We pair them with an audiobook, a series, or a YouTube video playing on your phone. Cozy games don't drain your energy as action-based shooters or especially soul like games do. Instead, they let you rest while you play.

**Для Напарника:** Фокус как низкоэнергетическое сопровождение: можно делать дело под фоновое присутствие кота и костра, не требуя усилий.



# 🎬 The three ways that good design makes you happy | Don Norman — TED


### Приятное работает лучше: тревога даёт depth-first, радость даёт breadth-first  ·  [03:54](https://youtu.be/RlQEoJaLQRA?t=234)
**Мысль:** Страх и тревога сужают фокус (depth-first), а лёгкий позитив с дофамином в префронтальной коре делает мышление широким и творческим (опыт Элис Айсен со свечой конфет). Для работы нужен дедлайн, для идей хорошее настроение.

> I really have the feeling that pleasant things work better, and that never made any sense to me until I finally figured out -- look ... I'm going to put a plank on the ground. So, imagine I have a plank about two feet wide and 30 feet long and I'm going to walk on it, and you see I can walk on it without looking, I can go back and forth and I can jump up and down. No problem. Now I'm going to put the plank 300 feet in the air -- and I'm not going to go near it, thank you. Intense fear paralyzes you. It actually affects the way the brain works. So, Paul Saffo, before his talk said that he didn't really have it down until just a few days or hours before the talk, and that anxiety was really helpful in causing him to focus. That's what fear and anxiety does; it causes you to be -- what's called depth-first processing -- to focus, not be distracted. And I couldn't force myself across that. Now some people can -- circus workers, steel workers. But it really changes the way you think. And then, a psychologist, Alice Isen, did this wonderful experiment. She brought students in to solve problems. So, she'd bring people into the room, and there'd be a string hanging down here and a string hanging down here. It was an empty room, except for a table with a bunch of crap on it -- some papers and scissors and stuff. And she'd bring them in, and she'd say, "This is an IQ test and it determines how well you do in life. Would you tie those two strings together?" So they'd take one string and they'd pull it over here and they couldn't reach the other string. Still can't reach it. And, basically, none of them could solve it. You bring in a second group of people, and you say, "Oh, before we start, I got this box of candy, and I don't eat candy. Would you like the box of candy?" And turns out they liked it, and it made them happy -- not very happy, but a little bit of happy. And guess what -- they solved the problem. And it turns out that when you're anxious you squirt neural transmitters in the brain, which focuses you makes you depth-first. And when you're happy -- what we call positive valence -- you squirt dopamine into the prefrontal lobes, which makes you a breadth-first problem solver: you're more susceptible to interruption; you do out-of-the-box thinking. That's what brainstorming is about, right? With brainstorming we make you happy, we play games, and we say, "No criticism," and you get all these weird, neat ideas. But in fact, if that's how you always were you'd never get any work done because you'd be working along and say, "Oh, I got a new way of doing it." So to get work done, you've got to set a deadline, right? You've got be anxious. The brain works differently if you're happy. Things work better because you're more creative. You get a little problem, you say, "Ah, I'll figure it out." No big deal.

**Для Напарника:** Тёплый тон Напарника помогает искать решение мелких проблем; для старта задачи можно держать лёгкую «приятность», а не тревожный дедлайн.



# 🎬 The UX Psychology Behind Apps People Can’t Stop Using — uxpeak


### Умолчания вместо пустых форм: decision fatigue  ·  [00:22](https://youtu.be/2TlIg3VokY8?t=22)
**Мысль:** Каждое пустое поле это решение; опыт с джемом: 24 вкуса дали 3% покупок, 6 вкусов 30%. 70-90% пользователей не меняют дефолты, воспринимая их как рекомендацию; задача превращается из «заполни с нуля» в «поправь».

> That sounds obvious, but almost nobody does. Stop giving users blank forms. Look at this booking screen. Five completely empty fields. Every single one is a decision the user has to make before anything happens. Psychologists call this decision fatigue. The more choices you stack on someone at once, the more likely they are to make no choice at all and just leave. A study from Columbia University found that when a grocery store displayed 24 jam flavors, only 3% of people bought one. When they showed just six, the purchase rate jumped to 30%. More choices doesn't mean better. It means harder. Now look at this version. Same screen, same fields, but everything is pre-filled with the most common choices. And the button doesn't just say search. It tells you there are already 12 results waiting. Here's why this works so well. 70 to 90% of users in most products never change the default values. That's not laziness. It's trust. When you provide a smart default, users read it as a recommendation. You're saying, "This is what most people pick." And that's incredibly persuasive. The user's job shifts from "Fill this out from scratch." to "Scan and adjust what doesn't fit." That's a fundamentally easier task. So, the principle is called smart defaults. And the rule is simple. Pre-select the most common choice for every field. Don't make users think when you already know the answer.

**Для Напарника:** Подтверждает Default Effect в Фокусе: 25 минут предвыбрано как рекомендуемое; предзаполнять шаг/длительность по прошлой сессии.


### Эффект цели: не начинай с нуля (карточка 10 штампов, 2 уже стоят)  ·  [01:41](https://youtu.be/2TlIg3VokY8?t=101)
**Мысль:** В автомойке карточка с двумя уже поставленными штампами завершалась почти вдвое чаще; прогресс 20% с первым шагом отмеченным вместо 0% даёт ощущение импульса (LinkedIn profile strength).

> This tip will change the way you think about onboarding entirely. Researchers at Columbia ran a study at a car wash. One group of customers got a loyalty card with eight empty stamps. Fill all eight, get a free wash. The second group got a card with 10 stamps, but two were already filled in. Same eight washes needed, but the group that started with two stamps completed the card at nearly double the rate. That's the goal gradient effect. The closer people feel to finishing something, the faster they move toward it. And the critical insight is this, you can choose where the starting line is. Look at this onboarding screen. 0% complete, five empty stamps. It's telling the user you haven't started and there's a lot ahead. That's deflating, even if the actual work is minimal. Now, this version. Same form, same fields, but progress already shows 20%. The first step is checked off. The user can see exactly where they are and how close the finish line is. They didn't do any extra work. You just reframed account creation as step one instead of treating it as a separate event. 0% feels like standing still. 20% feels like momentum. And that feeling is what separates the users who finish onboarding from the ones who drop off at the second screen. LinkedIn does this brilliantly. They show you a profile strength meter from the moment you sign up, and it's never at zero. The rule, never start a user at zero. Find something they've already done and count it. That artificial head start creates real motivation.

**Для Напарника:** Не показывать пустой остров/счётчик: засчитывать вход в сессию или решение начать как первый «штамп»; ноль подавать как «ноль — это не минус».

This is an SOS button. And it'll basically ask you how you're feeling based on either wanting to feel okay in the moment or wanting to face the panic attack head-on. And it walks you through these prompts which help to essentially activate the parasympathetic nervous system. And the parasympathetic nervous system is the one that calms us down.

**Мысль:** Социальные последствия всегда отложены; решение - искусственные близкие последствия и внешняя подотчётность.

# 🎬 TikTok Is Stealing Your Time — Yondoo Explains


### Скука как сигнал: сигнал, привычка, награда  ·  [08:34](https://youtu.be/w0xpr4ItRvA?t=514)
**Мысль:** Привычка — выученная связь между ситуацией и поведением: скука запускает телефон, награда — стимуляция.

> Boredom. Now, here's something people underestimate. You don't always open TikTok because you desperately want TikTok. Sometimes you're just bored. Waiting for your food, standing in line, sitting on the bus, waiting for a friend, lying in bed. 5 seconds of empty time appears. Your hand reaches for your phone. This can become a habit. A habit is a learned pattern where a repeated behavior becomes associated with a particular cue or situation. The cue might be boredom. The response is checking your phone. The reward is stimulation. And eventually, you don't need to consciously think, "I am now going to consume short-form video." Your hand just does it. Your brain has already learned the routine.

**Для Напарника:** Подменить рутину: скука/застревание → открыть Напарника, и получить маленькое приятное действие.


### Среда побеждает дисциплину: трение работает без монаха в пещере  ·  [09:39](https://youtu.be/w0xpr4ItRvA?t=579)
**Мысль:** Печенье на столе заставляет решать каждый раз, печенье в магазине — самоконтроль за тебя; трение снижает автоматику.

> The problem with just have discipline. This is where people often give terrible advice. Just use more discipline. Okay, very inspiring. But discipline isn't the only variable. Your environment matters. If your phone is sitting next to your keyboard with notifications constantly appearing, resisting it requires repeated decisions. If the phone is in another room, the behavior requires more effort. That's a completely different situation. Think about food. If there is a box of cookies sitting directly on your desk, you have to repeatedly decide not to eat them. If the cookies are in a store 20 minutes away, congratulations. Your environment just developed self-control for you. The same principle applies to attention. Friction. Friction means the amount of effort required to perform an action. Make something easier and people generally do it more. Make something harder and people generally do it less. Not always, but often enough to matter. Want to reduce random phone checking? You can increase friction. Turn off unnecessary notifications. Move distracting apps away from your home screen. Log out. Put your phone somewhere less convenient. Use app limits if they actually help you. None of this requires becoming a monk who lives in a cave. You are simply changing the environment.

**Для Напарника:** Подсказка «убери телефон подальше на время сессии» кота; блок отвлечений как мягкое предложение.

> It's that first video that immediately starts playing as soon as you open the app you see unlike other video services that you use like youtube or netflix tick tock starts the chain reaction of viewing straight away without your consent now with youtube if i want somebody to watch my video i as a creator have to give you guys a reason to click on my video it's not a very complicated task but it's what in behavioral science we might call a decision point it's a brief limbo in the user experience where the user has to decide what kind of video they're in the mood to watch and has to compare all the different titles and thumbnails that are vying for their attention the design of tiktok is so aggressively frictionless that they even remove this very first decision point by tik tok launching you straight into a video that they're pretty sure you're going to like you immediately become rewarded for opening the app and this leads to a positive reinforcement that makes you more likely to do it more and more in the future you can think of it a bit like a rat in a skinner box push the lever and get the food open the tik tok app and get a rewarding video almost immediately.

**Для Напарника:** Фокус: при входе сразу готовый шаг/таймер, а не выбор; уменьшать число решений до начала (Hick, default effect).



# 🎬 Time Blindness & ADHD Explained | Stop Being Late — Chris Blundell


### Казино убирают часы и окна, а СДВГ-день ощущается так же  ·  [01:17](https://youtu.be/u9bPfLKuJgE?t=77)
**Мысль:** Казино намеренно лишают чувства времени; у людей с СДВГ погружение в ритм без часов даёт то же ощущение утекающего дня.

> So if you think about Las Vegas and the casinos that they actually do everything they can to promote time blindness in all of the people that go there in order to get them to spend more time doing what they're doing there which is spending more money and giving all their money away to the casinos right and what they do for example is they get rid of clocks and windows they manage people's movements to create a steady Rhythm and they give them free alcohol to impair their time perception and they enhance a comfort of being in that place with nice furniture warm lighting consistent temperature control that are used to lower that alertness what happens is the they do everything they can to make sure that you are not aware of what time it is how long you've been there Etc and that's how it feels to live with ADHD where you get into a rhythm of doing what you're doing and next thing you know time has slipped away and it's night time and you haven't done your work etc.

**Для Напарника:** Показывать течение времени мягко и видимо (небо по часу суток, кольцо); не быть казино-паттерном без часов.


### Время как «сейчас / не сейчас»: всё не-сейчас одинаково далеко  ·  [02:35](https://youtu.be/u9bPfLKuJgE?t=155)
**Мысль:** Дело на пять минут и на пять лет равно «не сейчас», поэтому приоритизация сложна.

> Another way to look at time blindness is this all or nothing thinking this now or not now feeling that we have where whatever task we're doing is what's happening right now and that's where our focus is everything else whether it's five minutes away or five years away is not now so it's split right down the middle this is what I'm doing which is the now and that's what I'm not doing which is the not now so it could be a challenge to figure out prioritization.

**Для Напарника:** Превращать далёкое в ближайший шаг («сейчас» = 5 минут) и показывать его на Доме.


### Среда видимого времени и дробление задач проявляют их реальную цену  ·  [03:28](https://youtu.be/u9bPfLKuJgE?t=208)
**Мысль:** Часы, окна и визуальные напоминания делают время видимым; разбиение на мелкие задачи показывает, сколько всего в одной.

> Some examples which is using physical clocks wearing watches spending time near Windows visual reminders basically creating an environment for us where we are constantly able to see the passage of time one of the things that I talk with my clients about is breaking things into smaller tasks because once you start to look at those tasks and estimate how long they take then you'll be surprised at how much actually is involved in that one particular task.

**Для Напарника:** Разбиение на микрошаги с оценкой минут для каждого.


### Оцени и замерь: 15 минут масла на деле 38  ·  [03:50](https://youtu.be/u9bPfLKuJgE?t=230)
**Мысль:** Расхождение оценки и факта копится каждый день и разрушает план; тренировать нужно навык оценки через замеры.

> Another thing is carrying around a kitchen sign which is something that I have here basically the idea is that if you try to estimate how long you think a task will take and then time how long it actually takes it's actually usually a very different figure so let's say you think it takes 10 minutes to clean your kitchen and it actually takes 15 or 20 minutes that's not very much in the small scale but if that happens over and over for people with ADHD that can compound and it means that we are unable to plan our day and it adds to that feeling of overwhelm and that knock-on effect means that those five minutes here and 10 minutes there add up and can take away a significant part of our day for example a client of mine one of the tasks was taking their car to the garage to get an oil change and in their mind they thought it would take 15 minutes because it's a quick oil change place but the fact is it actually took 38 minutes and in hindsight it makes a lot of sense but until we start training our brain to think in that way and to really sit down and plan and estimate how long things take for people with ADHD especially it can be very difficult.

**Для Напарника:** После шага спрашивать «сколько ушло?» и накапливать личную калибровку, показывать мягко, без оценок.


### Таймер обратного отсчёта и правило «умножь оценку на два»  ·  [04:48](https://youtu.be/u9bPfLKuJgE?t=288)
**Мысль:** Убывающее время видно и ощущаемо; двойной запас либо даёт бонусные минуты, либо покрывает ошибку.

> Another way of combating it is actually using the timer in the opposite way so it can comes down so for example if you want to spend 20 minutes cleaning your kitchen rather than timing it upwards and counting to see if it does take 20 minutes start it at 20 minutes and count it down so that you're more aware and keep that time with you as you're doing that task so as you're doing it you can visually see that time counting down and get a better idea of how long things actually take one other thing that we can do is spend time in the morning planning out your day and as you're planning out that day on your calendar or whatever it is that you're using for however long you think a task will be double it so if you think something's going to take 10 minutes give yourself 20 minutes to do it the thinking behind this is let's say that you think a task takes 10 minutes and it does take 10 minutes but you've given yourself 20 minutes to do it that's great that means you have a spare 10 minutes to start the next task but if you're wrong and you think it takes 10 minutes and it does take 20 minutes then you've accounted for that time blindness.

**Для Напарника:** Таймер Фокуса — обратный отсчёт; при планировании добавлять запас (буфер) и подавать как подарок времени.



# 🎬 Time Blindness - An ADHD or Executive Dysfunction Trait — Therapy in a Nutshell


### Метафора корабля: слепота времени = не чувствовать приближающееся будущее  ·  [01:09](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=69)
**Мысль:** Нейротипичные «чувствуют» корабль издали и готовятся; при слепоте времени виден только уже накрывший тень; будущее ощущается только когда оно стало настоящим.

> Of having symptoms of time blindness is not being able to sense the future. so for example, let's imagine that you're standing at a dock watching a ship come to port. And in the distance you can see a huge ship on the horizon, and it's slowly approaching. You can sense that it's far off, and you have time before it arrives at the dock, so you go about preparing for its arrival as well as getting some other tasks done. As it gets closer, you sense some anticipation as you start to go about your work to receive the ship. And then it gets even closer, and you sense like you need to focus because it's about to arrive. And then the shadow of the ship covers you, and now you sense like it's time to take action. You've already observed and prepared, and now you need to help the ship come up to the dock. So you see how the word "sense" is used to describe this impending event of the ship coming into dock? You "sense" the future event, and it prompts you to take action ahead of time and make plans so that this big thing doesn't just come crashing in on you all at once. Well, for people with time blindness, that crash is exactly what happens. It's like they're on the docks with us too, but they don't really see the ship until the shadow is already cast over them. Or perhaps they do see it, but they just can't sense its slow movement towards them like others can. So this inability to sense the future makes it difficult or sometimes even impossible for people within the time-blind group to plan for the future. they don't sense the future until it's happening right now in the present, and then by then it's too late.

**Для Напарника:** Приближать будущее к настоящему: показывать ближайший шаг и награду сейчас, а не далёкие цели.


### Эксперимент Баркли с лампочкой: время воспринимается, но не воспроизводится  ·  [03:28](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=208)
**Мысль:** Люди с СДВГ оценивают прошедшее время так же, но не могут воспроизвести интервал сами: сбой в управлении действием по времени.

> Let's look at a fascinating study, the light bulb experiment. So Dr. Russell Barkley, a leading expert on ADHD, conducted an experiment to explore time perception in individuals with and without ADHD. He put participants in a room and showed them a light bulb. And he would turn it on for a time and then off again. And he'd asked them to estimate how long it had been on. And he found something interesting: both the ADHD and the non-ADHD participants were able to estimate the amount of time the light was on in about the same range of accuracy. But here's where things fell apart. For the participants with ADHD, when Dr. Barkley gave them the light switch and asked them to reproduce the time intervals themselves, the ADHDers were not able to reliably reproduce the same time interval with the light as the group with no ADHD symptoms. And so ADHDers were less capable of using their mind to govern their actions around time sensitive tasks. They could perceive time to a varying degree, but they could not judge or effectively estimate the amount of time an event or task would take for them to complete. So the time blind have more difficulty with managing the time related task that others find simple.

**Для Напарника:** Не просить пользователя «оценить время на глаз»; давать внешние визуальные опоры.


### Мир путает слепоту времени с ленью, хотя человек переживает сильнее других  ·  [04:47](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=287)
**Мысль:** Люди с слепотой времени очень заботятся и сурово судят себя за опоздания, возникает стыд.

> I think it's also important to recognize that the world at large confuses timed blindness with being lazy or not caring, and in most in most cases, the opposite is true: they care very much, and they're very hard on themselves when they constantly show up late to an appointment or they let someone down again. And they constantly ask themselves like, "Why can't I do this? Why am I always late? Why can't I get things done? How come everyone else just seems to be able to do this?" And there's this real sense of shame for many people who experience time blindness, and it's made worse when others don't understand this issue.

**Для Напарника:** Тёплая рамка для текстов: опоздание/срыв не повод для упрёка; в Напарнике нет красных «опоздал».


### Визуальный таймер показывает время без арифметики  ·  [06:26](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=386)
**Мысль:** Цифры и математика не работают, а оставшаяся доля времени видна сразу: «потрачена половина», «осталась полоска».

> A visual timer can help you feel how much time is left for a task. So instead of having to use the numbers and math, you can get the sense of, "Oh, I've used half my time" or "I've only got a sliver of time left.".

**Для Напарника:** Кольцо прогресса в Фокусе как визуальный таймер; крупная доля заполненного, без тревожных цифр.


### Замерь реальное время: «5 минут» на обувь оказалось 17, значит начинать в 7:43  ·  [07:38](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=458)
**Мысль:** Секундомер на повторяющихся задачах даёт реалистичную оценку; её переносят в напоминание.

> To make time visible for tasks or routines is to record them. So especially with tasks where you're chronically late, rushed, or taking too long to do something, get out your stopwatch and record the actual time it took to complete the task. If you do this a handful of times for the same task, you'll eventually be able to estimate the actual time it takes to get them done. So for example, if you're chronically showing up late to drop your kids off at school, time how long it takes for them to get ready, dressed, shoes on, and in the car with their seat belts on. I would even include how long it takes you to get down your driveway. So maybe you used to think that it takes 5 minutes for your kids to put their shoes on, but after timing it you've learned that it actually takes 17. Now you have a much more realistic idea that to get them out of the door by 8:00 they have to start putting their shoes on at 7:43. So put this number on a post-it note on your fridge, set an Alexa reminder, whatever it is that's going to help you get the actual task done.

**Для Напарника:** Копить фактическую длительность шагов и предлагать «обычно у тебя уходит около N минут».


### Сократи ожидание: дроби будущее событие на цепочку близких мини-событий  ·  [08:41](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=521)
**Мысль:** Далёкий дедлайн «не существует»; надо переносить исполнимые части в воспринимаемое настоящее и давать им срочность (оценка за этап).

> Shorten your wait times. Make it now. So people with time blindness don't feel the coming future. They only feel the present. And because waiting is very uncomfortable, they distract themselves, or they move on to some other project to escape that discomfort. So then from their perspective, that far off ambiguous future deadline suddenly arrives, and they didn't - they become overwhelmed because they didn't prepare for it. So the way we fix this - and this comes from Dr. Barkley again - is to lessen the amount of time a person has to wait between now and future events. And that's done by breaking the future event into smaller events that can be chained together so that it feels like the person has something to work on in the very near future. So for a student with time blindness, a project that's due in 3 months might as well not even exist. But breaking that project up into subtasks and milestones will give them something that they can continuously work on now and in the near future. it could be really helpful for teachers to add a grade to each milestone to add an extra sense of urgency to help motivate that student. it isn't just about breaking the task down, but it's actually about bringing actionable parts of that task into the perceivable present of the person with time blind.

**Для Напарника:** Ключевой обоснователь /api/breakdown: нарезать задачу так, чтобы ближайший шаг сегодня и сейчас.


### Закончи подготовку за полчаса до выхода: можно опоздать, не опаздывая  ·  [10:01](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=601)
**Мысль:** Буфер времени превращает неизбежное «чуть позже» в нормальный приход вовремя.

> I think Jessica McCabe from the channel How to ADHD put it best. She says, "I try to finish my morning routine half an hour before I leave for work, and that way I can run late without actually being late.".

**Для Напарника:** Подсказка перед важным делом: «поставим старт раньше на 15 минут для запаса».


### Плейлист вместо таймера: смена песни = маркер времени  ·  [11:13](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=673)
**Мысль:** Песни дают естественные отметки прохождения времени и приятнее сухого сигнала.

> Try using playlists to help you manage time. So let's say you only have 5 minutes before you need to leave the house. Instead of a timer, try playing a couple of songs back to back. When the first song ends and the second one starts, you'll know that you're halfway through your time. if you have a morning routine like you need to get through or a study session that needs to be a specific amount of time, use a playlist instead of an alarm. So it does take a little bit of preparation, but it's a fun way to keep yourself on track.

**Для Напарника:** Опциональный звуковой ритм сессии: мягкий сигнал на половине вместо тревожного будильника.


### Переведи минуты в секунды: «300 секунд» ощущается как настоящее  ·  [13:05](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=785)
**Мысль:** Секунды понятны человеку, поэтому 5 минут, выраженные в секундах, становятся ощутимыми, и он начинает реалистично планировать.

> Can see her realization light up. And those with time blindness don't perceive the future. They live in the now. So reframing 5 minutes into seconds allowed Roxanne to perceive 5 minutes as if it were the present because she already understands how long seconds are. And so now, because she could feel the 5 minutes in terms of the present, she was able to envision all of the activities that she could realistically accomplish in that time frame as opposed to her previous concept of 5 minutes, which did not work for her.

**Для Напарника:** Эксперимент для UI: показывать оставшееся время в ощущаемых единицах («пара песен», «душ») рядом с цифрой.


### Привяжи интервал к знакомому делу: 15 минут = дорога в магазин и обратно  ·  [13:41](https://youtu.be/wk_iX7pXGwk?t=821)
**Мысль:** Знакомый временной ориентир помогает понять, что реально сделать за заданное время.

> Minutes, which did not work for her. So how can you reframe time? How can you alter your perception to make these time intervals feel more immediate? here's an example that works for my husband. There is a little convenience store not far from here, and when he craves a quick snack he'll drive there and back. It doesn't take very long, but he's timed it and it seems to take 15 minutes round trip. So if he couldn't grasp the concept of 15 minutes on its own, he would use this, you know, familiar use of 15 minutes to really understand what he could get done in 15 minutes. Could I take a shower in the time it takes to go to the store and back? Yeah. Could I paint the walls of the room? probably not.

**Для Напарника:** Подписи длительностей в Фокусе через бытовые ориентиры («как сходить за чаем»).



# 🎬 Tiny Habits: B=MAP Behavior Model & Dark Patterns — Cognitive Dose


### B=MAP: убрать моральный груз и сделать действие крошечным  ·  [01:57](https://youtu.be/wVUuikXjFlE?t=117)
**Мысль:** Фогг снимает с провала привычки моральный вес: поведение происходит, когда сходятся мотивация, способность и триггер. Мотивацию (обезьянку) обходят, делая действие крошечным и привязывая к якорю.

> Culturally, we are completely saturated with shame around our habits, right? When someone fails to stick to a diet, society tells them they just lack willpower. >> Yeah, a personal moral failing. >> Exactly. But Fog, FOG, fundamentally removes that moral weight. He introduces the Fog, FOG, behavior model, which states that behavior happens when three things converge: motivation, ability, and a prompt. So, B = MAP. >> Right. And what makes this unique is how he treats the M, the motivation. >> Yeah, he calls it the motivation monkey. Because it's completely volatile. You might feel highly motivated to run 5 miles after watching an inspiring documentary, but, you know, that motivation just craters the next morning when your alarm goes off. >> It's too tied to shifting emotional states. >> Exactly. So, the framework brilliantly tells us to essentially engineer it out of the equation. You make the ability incredibly tiny, like doing two push-ups or flossing just one tooth. So, it takes less than 30 seconds. >> And then you use an existing anchor moment as the prompt. >> Right, like brushing your teeth. But here is the final crucial step. After the tiny behavior, the user has to immediately perform an artificial celebration. >> A fist pump or saying awesome out loud. >> Yeah, to create a feeling of shine. The book claims this creates an instant dopamine rush that wires the habit into the brain, bypassing the need for long-term repetition. The prime example is the Maui habit, waking up and immediately saying, "It's going to be a great day.".

**Для Напарника:** Шаг в Напарнике делать микроскопическим («открой файл»), привязывать к якорю дня и давать небольшое тёплое подтверждение без вины за пропуск.



# 🎬 Top 5 Advanced UX/UI Design Tips and Tricks Every Designer Needs to Know About – Part 3 — uxpeak


### Момент намерения: вместо пустого экрана предложи недавнее, популярное, персональное  ·  [02:26](https://youtu.be/Xzh8xjimmp8?t=146)
**Мысль:** Тап в поиск это момент намерения. Подсказки (недавние, популярные, персональные) не мешают тому, кто знает, что ищет, и снижают трение для неуверенных.

> Think about what happens when a user taps into a search bar. That's a moment of intent. They're looking for something, but they may not know exactly what. That's where your design can do one of two things. Give them a blank screen and expect them to do all the work. Or offer helpful suggestions that make the experience easier, faster, and smarter. Let's look at the first version, the standard blank screen search. Technically, it works, but there's no guidance. It's a dead end unless they know exactly what they want. Now, let's look at a smarter version of the same screen. The search bar is still there, ready to use, but underneath it, the interface offers subtle, useful suggestions. Things like recent searches, so the user can quickly pick up where they left off. Popular items, which gives them a sense of what others are finding useful. Personalized recommendations based on their past behavior or interest. These suggestions don't get in the way. They quietly support the user. And if they already know what they want, they can ignore them and just type. But if they're unsure, these small hints reduce friction and give them somewhere to start.

**Для Напарника:** Пустое поле шага на Фокусе предлагать с подсказками: недавние шаги и следующий шаг из очереди (Zeigarnik), чтобы не начинать с чистого листа.


### Метод ввода зависит от частоты: разовый выбор колесом, частый ввод текстом  ·  [08:33](https://youtu.be/Xzh8xjimmp8?t=513)
**Мысль:** Для разовой настройки уместны колесо и ползунок, для частого и точного ввода нужны поля и степперы. Важно снижать трение, а не выбирать красивое.

> First, consider the type of input. There are two kinds of numeric input scenarios. onetime setup like entering your weight when first signing up or setting your age, height or weight and frequent repeated entry like locking food to track calories or macros. These are completely different use cases and they require completely different UI patterns. Let's look at the screen where the user is asked to enter personal details like weight or height. In these cases, scroll wheels and sliders work beautifully. Why? Because users aren't entering this data often. They just want to get through the setup quickly and these values usually fall within a known range. And most importantly, they are low effort. Users don't need to type or think too much. Now, let's compare that to logging your food every day to track calories or macros. This is where sliders and scroll wheels break down. They're too slow, too fiddly, and don't offer the kind of control users need. If you want to log food quantity like 350 g, I don't want to scroll endlessly or try to drag a slider to the perfect number. Text fields are better here because they're faster, more precise, and less frustrating when used repeatedly. So, how do you choose? Here is a simple rule of thumb. Use sliders or scroll wheels for casual one-time inputs where precision isn't critical. Use text fields, steppers, or number inputs for frequent, precise, or repetitive tasks. It's not about which component looks nicer. It's about reducing friction and matching the input method to the context.

**Для Напарника:** Длительность на Фокусе выбирать чипами (быстро, редко меняется), а текст шага вводить полем; для минут избегать скролл-колёс.



# 🎬 Top 🔟 Executive Function skills To turn it around — Seth Perler, Executive Function, ADHD, 2e


### Мы лжём себе про время: «сделаю за пять минут» растягивается навсегда  ·  [03:40](https://youtu.be/Vyzgx0mpS1E?t=220)
**Мысль:** Самообман про длительность и «сделаю потом» - привычка, которую нужно осознанно ломать самонаблюдением.

> I told myself oh i can get this assignment done in five minutes oh i'll get it done tomorrow oh i'll get it done later tonight oh i'll put procrastinate on it but i'll get it done and i would lie to myself and i would think it would be easy and i would think i would just get it done and it would take me forever so being able to be self-aware is a skill because we want it's easier to just lie to ourselves until it's not easier to lie to yourself.

**Для Напарника:** Мягкая сверка оценки с фактом после сессии («ты думала 5, вышло 15») без оценки.


### Планируй с запасом: вместо часа на подготовку закладывай два  ·  [05:30](https://youtu.be/Vyzgx0mpS1E?t=330)
**Мысль:** Мы систематически недооцениваем время и энергию на домашку, поэтому удвоение оценки компенсирует ошибку.

> I learned to have certain planning skills like one of them was to over plan so i would think oh i'm gonna work on i'm gonna plan a half hour to study for this test well i learned that i really needed to plan two hours to study for a test to one hour because i would lie to myself or i would mis-estimate how long things would be so i learned to overplay and that was a skill.

**Для Напарника:** Предлагать длительность с запасом и дать возможность «продлить» сессию без ощущения провала.


### Не жди мотивации: таймеры, подотчётность и мелкие куски обманом запускают старт  ·  [06:22](https://youtu.be/Vyzgx0mpS1E?t=382)
**Мысль:** Мотивации к нелюбимому не бывает, поэтому старт надо технически провоцировать: таймер, другой человек, кусок на 5-15 минут.

> Number seven is self-starting skills i am a procrastinator i do not feel motivated to start things so i had to learn how do you self-start and what i thought i would do is i would wait until i was motivated i do not do that anymore i learned to not wait until i was motivated because that doesn't work i'm never motivated to do something i'm not motivated to do so i procrastinated a lot and i had to learn how to self-start i in doing that i learned to use timers i learned to use accountability that i'll talk about more in a minute i learned to chunk things down into small chunks that i could conceive of myself actually doing it you know five minutes 10 minutes 15 minutes of something rather than thinking you know i'm going to study for so i would study for long periods of time but in order to start i had to think of it as a smaller chunk so anyhow i had to build skills for starting skills for starting it's not just hey choose to start it's just you just need to motivate yourself i don't do that so i had to trick myself into starting.

**Для Напарника:** Подтверждение дизайна: короткий выбор длительности и напарник как триггер старта, а не вопрос «хочешь ли ты?».


### Системы нужно обслуживать: настроить один раз недостаточно  ·  [07:27](https://youtu.be/Vyzgx0mpS1E?t=447)
**Мысль:** Хорошо организованное разваливается без поддержки, как меч тупится, а машине нужна замена масла.

> Next number six maintenance skills one of my problems was i might set up a great system of folders i might set up my bedroom or office or study area in a great way i might set up you know organize all my papers or all my emails and get everything all organized while the problem was that i didn't maintain those organizational systems i didn't maintain my planners because i didn't maintain them they fell apart so what i had to do is learn that it's not just do it once and you're done like i have to maintain it's like sharpening a sword you know it gets dull so you have to maintain it.

**Для Напарника:** Периодический мягкий «подтюнь систему» в Доме (раз в неделю), а не требование идеально вести всё.


### Контрарное действие: первый импульс «отложу» нужно подвергнуть сомнению  ·  [10:40](https://youtu.be/Vyzgx0mpS1E?t=640)
**Мысль:** Первое решение при учёбе почти всегда избегание; осознанное действие наоборот ломает цепочку.

> To do what i call take contrary action meaning my first decision that i make in my mind usually is not a good one when it comes to school because my first decision is i'm gonna put it off i'm gonna procrastinate i'm gonna make excuses i'm gonna get my parents off my back my teacher's off my back i don't bug me i'm gonna my decision is i don't want to do this thing because i got more fun things to do but that was setting me up for a miserable life where i failed out of college dropped out of the next college i just didn't have a lot of opportunities and that did not work for me because i lied to myself so the decision making take contrary action do the opposite of what i think i should do my first instinct is no i don't want to do it do the opposite of those things.

**Для Напарника:** Когда человек колеблется перед стартом, напарник мягко предлагает «а что если попробуем 2 минуты?».


### Просить помощи сначала трудно, потом легко; нужна самоподдержка, а не стыд  ·  [12:28](https://youtu.be/Vyzgx0mpS1E?t=748)
**Мысль:** Эго мешает просить, но попросив несколько раз, человек понимает, что это просто; другие уже справились.

> Asking for help it's so hard to ask for help it was so hard for me to ask for help at first that got easy i didn't know it would be so easy until i tried it several times advocating for myself saying i care about myself i love myself i want to have a good life i don't care that my brain tells me not to do a bunch of stuff i'm going to figure this out i'm not going to let my mind hold me back with my resistance and my decision making i'm gonna make a good life for myself and i need help i cannot figure this out i don't know how to do this other people have figured it out i'm gonna ask people how do you do this.

**Для Напарника:** Внутри чата сделать «попросить помощь» лёгким и безобидным: кнопка «мне тяжело» вместо молчания.



# 🎬 UX/UI Design Review for Leading Healthcare Apps Headspace, Calm and Stoic — Komodo Digital


### Готовая временная лента занятий снимает бремя выбора в большой библиотеке  ·  [00:36](https://youtu.be/KIb99pO8ZKI?t=36)
**Мысль:** Когда человек пришёл успокоиться, не стоит заставлять его напрягаться: фиксированная последовательность активностей уменьшает нагрузку выбора.

> So headspace has a huge library of activities and video content which could be overwhelming to choose from especially if you're using the app for the first time so they've given us a set timeline of activities which reduces that burden of having to choose from a huge catalog I think when you when you come to an app with the goal of feeling more at ease is important not to have to strain your brain any more than is really necessary.

**Для Напарника:** Дом: предлагать одну следующую вещь вместо выбора из большого списка, Hick's Law, особенно для СДВГ.


### Показывай малый набор вариантов с возможностью добавить, а не всё сразу  ·  [12:35](https://youtu.be/KIb99pO8ZKI?t=755)
**Мысль:** Гибкая настройка хороша, но начинать лучше с небольшого набора и расширять, чем заставлять убирать лишнее; кнопка персонализации выглядела отключённой.

> The one thing I would say about the personalized button is that it kind of looks like it's in a disabled state but you can actually press it I think for me as well I would rather initially be served up a small amount of choices with the option to add more than to than to see everything off the bat and have to take away from it.

**Для Напарника:** Прогрессивное раскрытие: сначала три чипа длительности и пара шагов, остальное по запросу.
